Appendix A
Derivation of the interacting Hamiltonian for
\pi

In the main text, we make use of the standard operator form of the in-in formalism, which is formulated in the phase space. A crucial step is thus the derivation of the interacting Hamiltonian, which, however, is not simply equal to opposite the interacting Lagrangian in the case of time derivative interactions
[
71
,
50
,
72
]
. In this Appendix, we provide a detailed derivation of the interaction Hamiltonian for
\pi
used in the main text, paying particular attention to clarifying how the linear (
4.6
) and quadratic (
4.8
) interactions arise.

Since the derivation is already quite involved, it proves simpler to set
c_{s}=1
, i.e. to neglect the EFT operator
M^{4}(t+\pi)\left(\delta g^{00}\right)^{2}
, throughout this Appendix. This is justified by the fact that this operator does not bring any tadpole, and the quadratic and higher-order operators can be straightforwardly derived as usual without any ambiguity as discussed in Section
2.4
. Similarly, we also neglect the quadratic counterterms in Eq. (
4.9
).

Since the derivation is already quite involved, it proves simpler to consider only the term
2M_{2}^{4}(t+\pi)\dot{\pi}^{2}
in the EFT operator
M_{2}^{4}(t+\pi)\left(\delta g^{00}\right)^{2}/2
, as this is the only one that contributes to the dominant interactions—see discussion around Eqs. (
2.28
)-(
2.34
) in Section
2
. Similarly, we also neglect the quadratic counterterms in Eq. (
4.9
). This is justified by the fact that these are simple quadratic operators and the interacting Hamiltonian is simply related to the interacting Lagrangian by a minus sign without any ambiguity as discussed in Section
2.4
.

Our starting point is the following bare Lagrangian:

\displaystyle a^{3}\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\left(\Lambda_{\rm ren}(t+\pi)+\delta\Lambda(t+\pi)\right)

\displaystyle-a^{3}\left(c_{\rm ren}(t+\pi)+\delta c(t+\pi)\right)\left(-2\dot{\pi}-\dot{\pi}^{2}+\frac{(\partial\pi)^{2}}{a^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+2a^{3}\left(M_{\rm 2,\,ren}^{4}(t+\pi)+\delta M_{2}^{4}(t+\pi)\right)\dot{\pi}^{2},

(A.1)

where, as in the main text, we set
\Lambda_{\rm ren}\equiv 2\dot{H}+3H^{2}
,
c_{\rm ren}\equiv-\dot{H}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}
and
\frac{1}{c_{s}^{2}}-1=\frac{2M_{\rm 2,\,ren}^{4}}{\epsilon H^{2}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}}
. To arrive at the Hamiltonian, we first compute the momentum conjugate to
\pi
:

\displaystyle p_{\pi}\equiv\frac{\delta\,(a^{3}\mathcal{L})}{\delta\dot{\pi}}=

\displaystyle-2a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}(1+\dot{\pi})\left[\dot{H}(t+\pi)-\frac{\delta c(t+\pi)}{M_{\rm Pl}^{2}}\right]

(A.2)

\displaystyle+4a^{3}\dot{\pi}\left(M_{\rm 2,\,ren}^{4}(t+\pi)+\delta M_{2}^{4}(t+\pi)\right),

which we can invert as

\dot{\pi}[p_{\pi}]=\frac{p_{\pi}-2a^{3}\left[-\dot{H}(t+\pi)M_{\rm Pl}^{2}+\delta c(t+\pi)\right]}{2a^{3}\left[-\dot{H}(t+\pi)M_{\rm Pl}^{2}+\delta c(t+\pi)+2M_{\rm 2,\,ren}^{4}(t+\pi)+2\,\delta M_{2}^{4}(t+\pi)\right]}.

(A.3)

We note that Ref.
[
116
]
also pointed out the importance of terms linear in the field velocity
\dot{X}
in Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}[X,\,\dot{X}]
to correctly define the Hamiltonian.
In that paper, the author does not take an EFT approach and works within the single-field inflation Lagrangian for the curvature perturbation
\zeta
, adding by hand a counterterm of the form
a^{3}\mathcal{L}\supset 2a^{3}c(t)\,\dot{\zeta}
.
As a consequence, the presence of the linear term does not generate quadratic terms
in the Lagrangian
from the non-linearly realized symmetries of the EFT, which we will prove crucial for our result to be regular at late times.

We can now perform the Legendre transform

\mathcal{H}=p_{\pi}\dot{\pi}[p_{\pi}]-a^{3}\mathcal{L}

(A.4)

to get the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left\{p_{\pi}+2a^{3}\left[\dot{H}(t+\pi)M_{\rm Pl}^{2}-\delta c(t+\pi)\right]\right\}^{2}}{4a^{3}\left[-\dot{H}(t+\pi)M_{\rm Pl}^{2}+\delta c(t+\pi)+2M_{\rm 2,\,ren}^{4}(t+\pi)+2\,\delta M_{2}^{4}(t+\pi)\right]}

\displaystyle-a^{3}\left[\dot{H}(t+\pi)M_{\rm Pl}^{2}-\delta c(t+\pi)\right]\frac{(\partial\pi)^{2}}{a^{2}}

\displaystyle+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\left[2\dot{H}(t+\pi)+3H^{2}(t+\pi)\right]+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\,\delta\Lambda(t+\pi).

(A.5)

It is worth pausing a second to comment on this result. The Hamiltonian (
A.5
) is fully non-perturbative. While it is of little use in its current form, the advantage is the simplicity in its derivation, made possible by the fact that the relation
\dot{\pi}=\dot{\pi}[p_{\pi}]
can be inverted exactly. There is another feature of the Hamiltonian which may look unusual. As mentioned in the main text, (
2.2
) was imposed to ensure that the action vanishes at linear order action in the metric fluctuations, which is not evident from (
A.5
). Indeed, expanding it perturbatively, the Hamiltonian would contain both a term linear in
\pi
and one linear in
p_{\pi}
, that do not manifestly cancel. To see this cancellation at the Lagrangian level, we had to integrate by parts some of the terms in the action–see discussion around Eq. (
2.24
) in the main text. The equivalent operation at the level of the phase-space variables is a canonical transformation
[
36
]
:

\displaystyle p_{\pi}\mapsto

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{\pi},

\displaystyle\pi\mapsto

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\mapsto

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\left[\tilde{\pi},\tilde{p}_{\pi}\right]=\mathcal{H}\left[\pi(\tilde{\pi},\tilde{p}_{\pi}),p_{\pi}(\tilde{\pi},\tilde{p}_{\pi})\right]+\left.\frac{\partial F_{2}}{\partial t}\right|_{\pi(\tilde{\pi},\tilde{p}_{\pi}),\tilde{p}_{\pi},t},

(A.6)

where
F_{2}
is the generating function of the (type-2) canonical transformation.
To see the cancellation explicitly, we thus perform the following canonical transformation:

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{\pi}=

\displaystyle p_{\pi}+2a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\dot{H}(t+\pi)

(A.7)

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}=

\displaystyle\pi

(A.8)

\displaystyle F_{2}\left[\pi,\,\tilde{p}_{\pi},t\right]=

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{\pi}\pi-\int\differential\pi\,2a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\dot{H}(t+\pi).

(A.9)

Crucially, this transformation leaves
\pi
unaffected, which means that correlation functions of the original variable
\pi
computed using
\mathcal{H}
are exactly equal to correlation functions of
\tilde{\pi}
computed using
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
.
The transformed Hamiltonian is given by:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{H}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\left[\tilde{p}_{\pi}-2a^{3}\,\delta c(t+\pi)\right]^{2}}{4a^{3}\left[-\dot{H}(t+\pi)M_{\rm Pl}^{2}+\delta c(t+\pi)+2M_{\rm 2,\,ren}^{4}(t+\pi)+2\,\delta M_{2}^{4}(t+\pi)\right]}

\displaystyle-a^{3}\left[\dot{H}(t+\pi)M_{\rm Pl}^{2}-\delta c(t+\pi)\right]\frac{(\partial\pi)^{2}}{a^{2}}

(A.10)

\displaystyle+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\left[2\dot{H}(t+\pi)+3H^{2}(t+\pi)\right]+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\,\delta\Lambda(t+\pi)-2M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\int\differential\pi a^{3}\dot{H}(t+\pi).

We note that a similar result has been very recently obtained in Refs.
[
123
,
124
]
, which, however, did not consider counterterms and the operator
M_{2}^{4}
which generates a speed of sound
c_{s}\neq 1
. We recover their results by setting
\delta c=\delta\Lambda=M_{\rm 2,\,ren}^{4}=\delta M_{2}^{4}=0
.

We see that the counterterm
\delta c
effectively redefines the first SR parameter according to Eq. (
4.5
). Similarly, the counterterm
\delta M_{2}^{4}
redefines the speed of sound.
We are finally in the position to expand the Hamiltonian perturbatively in
\pi
.
Omitting the
\tilde{}
for simplicity, and keeping only the leading orders in the SR parameter
\eta
and
\eta_{2}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{s}^{2}\,p_{\pi}^{2}}{4a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}H^{2}\epsilon}\left(1-H\eta\pi+\frac{H^{2}}{2}\eta(\eta-\eta_{2})\pi^{2}\right)

(A.11)

\displaystyle+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}H^{2}\epsilon\left(1-H\eta\pi+\frac{H^{2}}{2}\eta(\eta+\eta_{2})\pi^{2}\right)\frac{(\partial\pi)^{2}}{a^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}}{2}\,\delta\ddot{\Lambda}(t)\,\pi^{2}-\left(\delta\dot{c}(t)\,-\,\eta H\,\delta c(t)\right)\frac{c_{s}^{2}\,p_{\pi}\pi}{M_{\rm Pl}^{2}H^{2}\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{s}^{2}\,p_{\pi}\,\delta c(t)}{M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\dot{H}}+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\,\delta\dot{\Lambda}(t)\pi-\frac{c_{s}^{4}\,\delta M_{2}^{4}}{8a^{6}H^{4}\epsilon^{2}M_{\rm Pl}^{4}}\,p_{\pi}^{2},

where we explicitly see that the only linear interactions are those induced by the counterterms
\delta c
and
\delta\Lambda
, whereas those induced by
a^{3}\mathcal{L}\supset-a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}[2\dot{H}(t+\pi)+3H^{2}(t+\pi)]-2a^{3}\dot{H}(t+\pi)\dot{\pi}
cancel out.

We can now define the free theory Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}_{\rm free}=\frac{c_{s}^{2}\,p_{\pi}^{2}}{4a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}H^{2}\epsilon}+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}H^{2}\epsilon\frac{\left(\partial\pi\right)^{2}}{a^{2}},

(A.12)

which governs the evolution of the interaction picture fields
p_{\pi}^{I}
and
\pi^{I}
.
Using the relation
p^{I}_{\pi}=2a^{3}H^{2}\epsilon M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\dot{\pi}^{I}/c_{s}^{2}
, we can finally get the interacting Hamiltonian expressed in terms of interaction picture fields and momenta:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\rm int}(\pi^{I},p_{\pi}^{I})=

\displaystyle-a^{3}H^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\epsilon\eta\,\pi^{I}\left[\frac{\left(\dot{\pi}^{I}\right)^{2}}{c_{s}^{2}}-\frac{\left(\partial\pi^{I}\right)^{2}}{a^{2}}\right]

(A.13)

\displaystyle+\frac{a^{3}}{2}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}H^{4}\epsilon\eta\,\left(\pi^{I}\right)^{2}\left[(\eta-\eta_{2})\frac{\left(\dot{\pi}^{I}\right)^{2}}{c_{s}^{2}}+(\eta+\eta_{2})\frac{\left(\partial\pi^{I}\right)^{2}}{a^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}}{2}\,\delta\ddot{\Lambda}(t)\,\left(\pi^{I}\right)^{2}-2a^{3}\left[\delta\dot{c}(t)\,-\,\eta H\,\delta c(t)\right]\dot{\pi}^{I}\pi^{I}

\displaystyle-2a^{3}\,\delta c(t)\dot{\pi}^{I}+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\,\delta\dot{\Lambda}(t)\pi^{I}+a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\epsilon H^{2}\delta_{c_{s}^{2}}\left(\dot{\pi}^{I}\right)^{2},

where we have defined
-\delta M_{2}^{4}/2\equiv a^{3}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\epsilon H^{2}\,\delta c_{s}^{2}
to match the notation in Eq. (
4.9
) in the main text.
We see that imposing the cancellation of the tadpole at one-loop—which generates a non-zero
\delta c
—allows us to preserve the background solution while simultaneously accounting for the effects of the quantum backreaction by simply redefining
\epsilon
according to Eq. (
4.5
). In other words, we incorporate the effects of backreaction by modifying the equations of motion for the free fields, whose mode functions are given by Eq. (
3.4
) with the redefined
\epsilon
given in Eq. (
4.5
).

Appendix B
Adding a mass in
3+\delta
dimensions

The main complication in solving the loop integrals in the main text is that the mode functions in
d\neq 3
spatial dimensions are given in the form of Hankel functions with a generic index
\nu
, which cannot be expressed in a simple form in terms of elementary functions. Recently, the authors of Ref.
[
73
]
introduced a method to get around this complication. They proposed adding a mass term to the action as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{X}\supset-\frac{M_{\rm Pl}^{2}}{X_{0}}\,\mu^{\delta}\int\,\differential\tau\,\differential^{3+\delta}x\,a^{2+\delta}(1-\varepsilon_{X})H^{2}\frac{(d^{2}-9)}{4}X^{2},

(B.1)

where,
X=\pi,\,\gamma,\,\mathcal{S}
and
X_{0}
is the correct normalization of the action in the three cases. Compared to Ref.
[
73
]
, we have added by hand a book-keeping parameter
\varepsilon_{X}
which we can set either to
0
or
1
depending on whether we want to use this method or not—as in the main text—respectively. The mass term explicitly vanishes when
\delta=0
, so that the fields are massless in 3 spatial dimensions. The advantage of keeping the mass term is that the index of the Hankel functions becomes

\nu_{X}^{2}\mapsto\nu_{X}^{2}-\frac{\varepsilon_{X}-1}{4}\left[\left(3+\delta\right)^{2}-9\right].

(B.2)

and the mode functions are given by

\displaystyle\pi_{k}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\pi}e^{i\pi\delta/4}c_{s}^{-(1+\delta)/2}}{2\sqrt{2\epsilon}}\frac{1}{M_{\rm Pl}}\left(\frac{H}{\mu}\right)^{\delta/2}\frac{(-c_{s}k\tau)^{(3+\delta)/2}}{k^{(3+\delta)/2}}H^{(1)}_{(3+\delta{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}})/2}(-c_{s}k\tau),

(B.3)

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\pi}e^{i\pi\delta/4}\frac{H}{M_{\rm Pl}}\left(\frac{H}{\mu}\right)^{\delta/2}\frac{(-k\tau)^{(3+\delta)/2}}{k^{(3+\delta)/2}}H^{(1)}_{(3+\delta{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\gamma}})/2}(-k\tau),

(B.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\pi}e^{i\pi\delta/4}c_{\mathcal{S}}^{-(1+\delta)/2}}{2}H\left(\frac{H}{\mu}\right)^{\delta/2}\frac{(-c_{\mathcal{S}}k\tau)^{(3+\delta)/2}}{k^{(3+\delta)/2}}H^{(1)}_{(1+\delta{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\mathcal{S}}})/2}(-c_{\mathcal{S}}k\tau).

(B.5)

If
\varepsilon_{X}=0
, the computation is thus greatly simplified, as the Hankel functions are given in terms of elementary functions and the only effect of dimensional regularization is to introduce a change in the integration measure and adding an extra
\tau^{\delta/2}
to the mode functions. In the following, we will quote the full results keeping track of the book-keeping parameters
\varepsilon_{X}
.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}H^{2}\frac{\eta(\eta-2\eta_{2})}{2}\log\left(\sqrt{\pi}\frac{H}{\mu}\right)-\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}}{2}H^{2}\,\eta(\eta-\eta_{2})\log(t_{\rm IR})

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}}{8}H^{2}\gamma_{\rm E}\eta\,\left[-13\eta+10\eta_{2}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}(14\eta-12\eta_{2})\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}}{16}H^{2}\log(2)\,\eta\,\left[-53\eta+16\eta_{2}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}(28\eta-24\eta_{2})\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}}{4}H^{2}\log(c_s)\,\eta\,\left[-6\eta+4\eta_{2}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}(7\eta-6\eta_{2})\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}}{480}H^{2}\,\eta\,\left[2717\eta-1140\eta_{2}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}(-1910\eta+1360\eta_{2})\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}}{4}H^{2}\log(x)\,\eta\,({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}-1)(7\eta-6\eta_{2})\,,

(C.3)

which shows from the last line that the only choice of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}
such that the renormalized power spectrum reaches a constant on super-horizon scales is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}=1
, which is the procedure followed in the main text, and not the one shown in App.
B
.

Tensor power spectrum.

\gamma
are given by the following expressions. When writing the results involving counterterms
\delta_{1}^{\gamma},\,\delta_{2}^{\gamma},\,\delta_{3}^{\gamma}
, we use their explicit expressions (
5.13
).

\displaystyle-2\real\frac{p^{3+\delta}\mu^{2\delta}}{8\pi^{2}}H^{4}\epsilon^{2}\int\frac{\differential^{3+\delta}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3+\delta}}\frac{1}{2}k^{4}\sin^{4}\theta\,\gamma_{p}^{2}(\tau)\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\>a^{2+\delta}(\tau_{1})\gamma^{*}_{p}(\tau_{1})\pi_{k}(\tau_{1})\pi_{q}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97733pt\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau_{1}}\differential\tau_{2}\>a^{2+\delta}(\tau_{2})\gamma_{p}^{*}(\tau_{2})\pi_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\pi_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\,

\displaystyle+\frac{p^{3+\delta}\mu^{2\delta}}{8\pi^{2}}H^{4}\epsilon^{2}\int\frac{\differential^{3+\delta}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3+\delta}}\frac{1}{2}k^{4}\sin^{4}\theta\,\absolutevalue{\gamma_p(\tau)}^{2}\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\>a^{2+\delta}(\tau_{1})\gamma^{*}_{p}(\tau_{1})\pi_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\pi_{q}^{*}(\tau_{1})

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.21051pt}{9.80649pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -8.59 13.57)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}=

\displaystyle-4\delta_{c_{s}^{2}}\epsilon H^{2}M_{\rm Pl}^{2}\imaginary\frac{p^{3+\delta}\mu^{\delta}}{2\pi^{2}}\,\int_{-\infty_{+}}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\>a^{2+\delta}(\tau_{1})\pi_{p}^{*2}(\tau)\,{\pi_{p}^{\prime}}^{2}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}\frac{\left(b^{(1,2)}\right)^{2}c_{\mathcal{S}}c_{s}^{3}(1-c_{s}^{2}x^{2})}{H^{2}}\biggl[\frac{1}{\delta}+\log\left(x\frac{H}{{\mu}}\right)\biggr]

\displaystyle+\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}\frac{\left(b^{(1,2)}\right)^{2}c_{\mathcal{S}}c_{s}^{3}}{H^{2}}\left[4-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}(1+c_{s}^{2}x^{2})\right]

\displaystyle-\,\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}\frac{\left(b^{(1,2)}\right)^{2}c_{\mathcal{S}}c_{s}^{3}}{H^{2}}\left[e^{-2ic_{s}x}(1+ic_{s}x)^{2}{\rm Ei}(2ic_{s}x)\,+\,{\rm c.c.}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}\frac{\left(b^{(1,2)}\right)^{2}c_{\mathcal{S}}c_{s}^{3}}{2H^{2}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}\Biggl[-ie^{-2ic_{s}x}(1+ic_{s}x)[\pi(-1-ic_{s}x+2c_{s}^{2}x^{2})

\displaystyle+(1+2ic_{s}x)(i+c_{s}x){\rm Ei}(2ic_{s}x)]+{\rm c.c.}\Biggr].

We now write explicitly the late-time limit of the renormalized correction to the scalar power spectrum from massive fields for general
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\epsilon_{\mathcal{S}}}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 0}\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,1{\rm L\,\,from}\,\,\mathcal{S}}^{\rm ren}(x)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\,0}^{\rm tree^{2}}\frac{b^{(1,2)}c_{s}}{8c_{\mathcal{S}}},

(C.7)

which shows it is independent on
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\gamma}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\mathcal{S}}}
.

By inspecting the corrections to the scalar and tensor power spectra, we conclude that in general it is not justified to take
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\pi}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{\gamma}}=0
, as in this case a spurious logarithmic divergence develops on super-horizon scales.

Appendix D
Momentum variables

Denoting the external and loop momenta respectively as
\vec{p}
and
\vec{k}
, and
\vec{q}=\vec{k}-\vec{p}
their difference, it is useful to define following, dimensionless variables

\displaystyle v=

\displaystyle\frac{k}{p}

(D.1)

\displaystyle u=

\displaystyle\frac{q}{p},

(D.2)

and recast the integral measure in
3+\delta
dimensions as

\displaystyle\int\differential^{3+\delta}k\,f(\vec{k})=

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi^{\frac{\delta+1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\delta+1}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential k\,k^{2+\delta}\,\int_{0}^{\pi}\differential\theta\,\sin^{1+\delta}\theta\int_{0}^{\pi}\differential\varphi\sin^{\delta}\varphi\,f(k,\,\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi^{\frac{\delta+3}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\delta+1}{2}\right)}\,p^{3+\delta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential v\,v^{1+\delta}\int_{\lvert 1-v\rvert}^{1+v}\differential u\,u\sin^{\delta}\theta\,f(u,v)

(D.3)

where we have assumed that the function
f
does not depend on the azimuthal angle, as is the case for the calculations that we are concerned with in this paper. However, working with (
D.3
) is not very convenient as the two integrals over
u
and
v
are nested, and, in particular, care must be taken in taking the large and small momenta limits of the integrand. It is therefore useful to decouple the two integrals by introducing the variables

\displaystyle u=

\displaystyle\frac{t+s+1}{2}

(D.4)

\displaystyle v=

\displaystyle\frac{t-s+1}{2},

(D.5)

so that the integral becomes

\frac{\pi^{\frac{\delta+3}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\delta+1}{2}\right)}\,p^{3+\delta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential t\,v^{1+\delta}\int_{-1}^{1}\differential s\,u\sin^{\delta}\theta\,f(s,t).

(D.6)

In particular, we have the relation:

\sin\theta=\sqrt{-\frac{\left(s^{2}-1\right)t(t+2)}{(-s+t+1)^{2}}}.

(D.7)

We also have the following useful relations:

\displaystyle\vec{p}\cdot\vec{k}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}p^{2}(st+s-1)

(D.8)

\displaystyle\vec{p}\cdot\vec{q}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}p^{2}(st+s+1)

(D.9)

\displaystyle\vec{k}\cdot\vec{q}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}p^{2}\left[s^{2}+t(t+2)-1\right].

(D.10)
